\begin{theorem}\label{thm:main}
The equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main}
2x^{4}+x+y^{3}+y+3=0
\end{equation}
has no integer solutions.
\end{theorem}

The proof combines quadratic Hilbert symbols over $\Q$ and cubic Hilbert symbols over $K=\Q(\omega)$, where $\omega^2+\omega+1=0$. To a hypothetical integer solution we attach the values of auxiliary polynomials $H_i$ with rational coefficients, linked by a graph, and the values of auxiliary polynomials $J_i$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$, linked by 2 weighted graphs. By the product formula and the reciprocity law, the products of the quadratic symbols over all places are $1$, and the sums of the cubic symbols over all places are $0$. We show that the local contributions vanish at the primes outside a finite set $S$, determine the sign contributions at the real place, compute the local contributions jointly at the primes of $S$, and obtain a contradiction. Apart from polynomial identities, which can be checked by expanding both sides, finite computations with residues, and basic properties of the Legendre symbol and of the quadratic Hilbert symbol, the proof uses two standard facts from class field theory: the explicit formula for the cubic Hilbert symbol at the places not above $3$, and the Hilbert reciprocity law.

\section{Legendre symbols and Hilbert symbols}

For a prime $p$ and a non-zero integer $N$, we write $v_p(N)$ for the exponent of $p$ in the prime factorization of $N$. For an integer $a$ and an odd prime $p$, the Legendre symbol $\leg{a}{p}$ is $0$ if $p\mid a$, $1$ if $a$ is a non-zero square modulo $p$, and $-1$ otherwise. It is multiplicative in $a$, and it depends only on $a$ modulo $p$ \cite[Chapter~5]{IR}.

For a prime $p$, let $\Q_p$ be the field of $p$-adic numbers, and put $\Q_\infty=\mathbb R$. For $v$ a prime or $v=\infty$, and non-zero $a,b\in\Q$, the Hilbert symbol $(a,b)_v$ is $1$ if the equation $z^2=ar^2+bs^2$ has a solution $(r,s,z)\neq(0,0,0)$ in $\Q_v$, and $-1$ otherwise. In particular, $(a,b)_v=1$ for every $v$ if this equation has a non-zero solution in $\Q$. For an odd integer $t$, we put $\varepsilon(t)=(t-1)/2$ and $\omega(t)=(t^2-1)/8$. We use the following facts \cite[Chapter~III, Proposition~2 and Theorems~1 and~3]{Se}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:hilbert}
Let $a$, $a'$ and $b$ be non-zero integers, and let $v$ be a prime or $v=\infty$.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $(a,b)_\infty=-1$ if and only if $a<0$ and $b<0$.
\item[(ii)] Let $p$ be an odd prime, and write $a=p^\alpha u$ and $b=p^\beta w$ with $p\nmid uw$. Then $(a,b)_p=(-1)^{\alpha\beta\varepsilon(p)}\leg{u}{p}^\beta\leg{w}{p}^\alpha$.
\item[(iii)] Write $a=2^\alpha u$ and $b=2^\beta w$ with $u$ and $w$ odd. Then $(a,b)_2=(-1)^{\varepsilon(u)\varepsilon(w)+\alpha\omega(w)+\beta\omega(u)}$.
\item[(iv)] $(a,b)_v=1$ for all but finitely many $v$, and $\prod_v(a,b)_v=1$, the product over all primes $v$ and $v=\infty$.
\item[(v)] $(a,b)_v=(b,a)_v$ and $(aa',b)_v=(a,b)_v(a',b)_v$.
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:square}
Let $a$ and $b$ be non-zero integers. If $p$ is an odd prime with $p\nmid a$ and $\leg{a}{p}=1$, then $(a,b)_p=1$. If $a\equiv1\pmod8$, then $(a,b)_2=1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first claim is Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(ii) with $\alpha=0$. For the second, $\varepsilon(a)$ and $\omega(a)$ are even, and Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(iii) with $\alpha=0$ gives $(a,b)_2=1$.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}\label{lem:classes}
Let $p$ be a prime. For a non-zero integer $X$, write $X=p^ju$ with $p\nmid u$. If $p$ is odd, put $c_p(X)=(j,e)\in\F_2^2$, where $\leg{u}{p}=(-1)^e$; if $p=2$, put $c_2(X)=(j,e_1,e_2)\in\F_2^3$, where $u\equiv(-1)^{e_1}5^{e_2}\pmod8$; all entries are taken modulo $2$. Then $c_p(XX')=c_p(X)+c_p(X')$ and $(X,X')_p=(-1)^{\beta_p(c_p(X),c_p(X'))}$ for non-zero integers $X,X'$, where $\beta_p$ is the symmetric bilinear form
\begin{align*}
\beta_p\bigl((j,e),(j',e')\bigr)&=jj'\varepsilon(p)+je'+j'e&&\text{if $p$ is odd},\\
\beta_2\bigl((j,e_1,e_2),(j',e_1',e_2')\bigr)&=e_1e_1'+je_2'+j'e_2.
\end{align*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For odd $p$, this is Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(ii), as the Legendre symbol is multiplicative. For $p=2$, the odd residues $1,3,5,7$ modulo $8$ are $(-1)^{e_1}5^{e_2}$ with $(e_1,e_2)=(0,0),(1,1),(0,1),(1,0)$, respectively; in each case $\varepsilon(u)\equiv e_1$ and $\omega(u)\equiv e_2\pmod2$, so Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(iii) gives the formula, and $c_2$ is additive because $(e_1,e_2)\mapsto(-1)^{e_1}5^{e_2}$ is an isomorphism from $\F_2^2$ to the group of odd residues modulo $8$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:residue}
Let $p$ be a prime, $k\geq1$, and let $X$ be a non-zero integer with $X\equiv r\pmod{p^k}$, where $0\leq r<p^k$. Then $c_p(X)\in b+D$ for the vector $b$ and the subspace $D$ given as follows. If $r=0$, then $b=0$ and $D$ is the whole space. Otherwise write $r=p^jr'$ with $p\nmid r'$, so that $j<k$. If $p$ is odd, or if $p=2$ and $k-j\geq3$, then $b=c_p(r)$ and $D=0$. If $p=2$ and $k-j=2$, then $b=(j,e_1,0)$ with $r'\equiv(-1)^{e_1}\pmod4$, and $D$ is spanned by $(0,0,1)$. If $p=2$ and $k-j=1$, then $b=(j,0,0)$, and $D$ is spanned by $(0,1,0)$ and $(0,0,1)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $r\neq0$, then $v_p(X)=j$ and $X/p^j\equiv r'\pmod{p^{k-j}}$. For odd $p$, the Legendre symbol of $X/p^j$ is therefore that of $r'$. For $p=2$, the residue of $X/2^j$ modulo $8$ is that of $r'$ if $k-j\geq3$, and its residue modulo $4$, which determines $e_1$, is that of $r'$ if $k-j=2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:envelope}
Let $p$ be a prime, and let $E$ be a set of edges on a finite set of vertices. For every vertex $i$, let $b_i$ be a vector and $D_i$ a subspace of $\F_2^2$ (of $\F_2^3$ if $p=2$), and put $\sigma_i=\sum_{j\sim i}b_j$, the sum over the edges $\{i,j\}\in E$. Suppose that $\beta_p(d,\sigma_i)=0$ for every vertex $i$ and every $d\in D_i$, and that $\beta_p(d,d')=0$ for every edge $\{i,j\}\in E$ and all $d\in D_i$ and $d'\in D_j$. Then
\[
\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\beta_p(c_i,c_j)=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\beta_p(b_i,b_j)\qquad\text{whenever $c_i\in b_i+D_i$ for every $i$.}
\]
By bilinearity, it suffices to check the hypotheses for $d$ and $d'$ in spanning sets of $D_i$ and $D_j$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $c_i=b_i+d_i$ with $d_i\in D_i$. As $\beta_p$ is bilinear and symmetric, the left side equals the right side plus $\sum_i\beta_p(d_i,\sigma_i)+\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\beta_p(d_i,d_j)$, and both sums vanish.
\end{proof}



\section{\texorpdfstring{Cubic Hilbert symbols over $\Q(\omega)$}{Cubic Hilbert symbols over Q(w)}}\label{sec:csymbols}

\paragraph{The field.} Let $K=\Q(\omega)$, where $\omega^2+\omega+1=0$. Its ring of integers is $\Z[\omega]$, and every element of $K$ can be written uniquely as $A+B\omega$ with $A,B\in\Q$. We write
\[
\langle A,B\rangle=A+B\omega,
\]
also when $A$ and $B$ are polynomials in $x$ and $y$. Then $\langle A,B\rangle\langle C,D\rangle=\langle AC-BD,\ AD+BC-BD\rangle$, and the norm is $N(\langle A,B\rangle)=A^2-AB+B^2$. In particular, a value $\langle A,B\rangle$ with $A,B\in\Z$ is zero if and only if $A=B=0$.

\paragraph{Places.} The finite places of $K$ correspond to the non-zero prime ideals of $\Z[\omega]$. A prime $p\equiv2\pmod3$ is inert: there is one place above $p$, with uniformizer $p$ and residue field $\Z[\omega]/p\Z[\omega]$ with $p^2$ elements. If $p\equiv1\pmod3$, the polynomial $t^2+t+1$ has two roots $r$ modulo $p$, and for each of them there is a place $v_r$ above $p$, at which $\omega$ is the root of $t^2+t+1$ in $\Z_p$ congruent to $r$ modulo $p$; the completion is $\Q_p$, the uniformizer is $p$, the residue field is $\F_p$, and $\omega$ reduces to $r$. The prime $3$ is ramified: $\pi=1-\omega$ generates the unique prime ideal above $3$, $\pi^2=-3\omega$, and $\Z[\omega]/\pi\Z[\omega]=\F_3$, where $\langle A,B\rangle$ reduces to $A+B$. The only infinite place of $K$ is complex. For a finite place $v$ we write $K_v$ for the completion and $v(a)$ for the normalized valuation, and we call $a$ a \emph{unit} at $v$ if $v(a)=0$.

\paragraph{Hilbert symbols.} For a place $v$ of $K$ and $a,b\in K_v^\times$, the cubic Hilbert symbol $(a,b)_v$ is a cube root of unity, see \cite[Chapter~V, \S3]{N}. We write $(a,b)_v=\omega^{\theta_v(a,b)}$ with $\theta_v(a,b)\in\Z/3\Z$, and we use the following three facts \cite[Chapter~V, \S3 and Chapter~VI, \S8]{N}.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(H1)] $\theta_v(a,bc)=\theta_v(a,b)+\theta_v(a,c)$, $\theta_v(a,b)=-\theta_v(b,a)$, and $\theta_v(a,c^3)=0$. In particular, $\theta_v(a,b)$ depends only on the classes of $a$ and $b$ in $K_v^\times/K_v^{\times3}$.
\item[(H2)] Let $v$ be a finite place not above $3$, let $q$ be the number of elements of its residue field, and let $\varpi$ be a uniformizer at $v$. Write $a=\varpi^eu$ and $b=\varpi^fw$ with units $u$ and $w$, and define $\chi(u)\in\Z/3\Z$ by $\bar u^{(q-1)/3}=\bar\omega^{\chi(u)}$, where the bar denotes the reduction to the residue field. Then $\theta_v(a,b)=e\,\chi(w)-f\,\chi(u)$. In particular, $\theta_v(a,b)=0$ if $a$ and $b$ are both units.
\item[(H3)] (Hilbert reciprocity.) For $a,b\in K^\times$, $\theta_v(a,b)=0$ for all but finitely many places $v$, and $\sum_v\theta_v(a,b)=0$, where the sum is over all places of $K$. At the complex place, $\theta_v(a,b)=0$.
\end{itemize}
The general formula in (H2) contains an additional term $ef\chi(-1)$, which vanishes because $-1=(-1)^3$ is a cube. Formula (H2) fixes the normalization of the symbols; with the opposite sign convention all values $\theta_v$ change sign, and the proof below is the same.

\paragraph{Classes at the places not above $3$.} Let $p\neq3$ be a prime and $v$ a place above $p$. For $a=p^eu\in K_v^\times$ with a unit $u$, put $\mathrm{cl}_v(a)=(e\bmod3,\ \chi(u))\in\F_3^2$. By (H2) and (H1), $\mathrm{cl}_v$ is additive, $\mathrm{cl}_v(a)$ determines the class of $a$ modulo cubes, and $\theta_v(a,b)=e\chi(w)-f\chi(u)$ for $\mathrm{cl}_v(a)=(e,\chi(u))$ and $\mathrm{cl}_v(b)=(f,\chi(w))$. We put $\mathrm{cl}_p(a)=\mathrm{cl}_v(a)\in\F_3^2$ if $p\equiv2\pmod3$, and $\mathrm{cl}_p(a)=(\mathrm{cl}_{v_r}(a),\mathrm{cl}_{v_{r'}}(a))\in\F_3^4$ if $p\equiv1\pmod3$, where $r<r'$ are the roots of $t^2+t+1$ in $\{1,\dots,p-1\}$; accordingly, $\theta_p$ is the sum of the forms $\theta_v$ over the places $v$ above $p$. For $p\equiv2\pmod3$ and a rational integer $u$ prime to $p$, we have $\bar u\in\F_p$, so that $\bar u^{(p^2-1)/3}=(\bar u^{p-1})^{(p+1)/3}=1$ and $\chi(u)=0$.

\paragraph{The place above $3$.}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cM3}
Let $v$ be the place above $3$. Every non-zero element of $K_v$ is equal to $\pi^a\omega^b2^c(1+3\omega)^d$ times a cube, for unique $a,b,c,d\in\Z/3\Z$, and every unit congruent to $1$ modulo $9$ is a cube. Put $\mathrm{cl}_3(A)=(a,b,c,d)\in\F_3^4$. Then $\mathrm{cl}_3$ is additive, and $\theta_v(A,B)=\mathrm{cl}_3(A)^{\mathsf T}M_3\,\mathrm{cl}_3(B)$, where
\[
M_3=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&2&0\\0&0&1&2\\1&2&0&0\\0&1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\pmod3.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $\alpha\in\Z[\omega]$, the polynomial $g(s)=s+3s^2+3s^3-\alpha$ satisfies $g'(s)\equiv1\pmod\pi$, so by Hensel's lemma it has a root $s$ in the completion of $\Z[\omega]$, and then $(1+3s)^3=1+9(s+3s^2+3s^3)=1+9\alpha$. Hence every unit congruent to $1$ modulo $9$ is a cube in $K_v$, and the class of a unit modulo cubes depends only on its residue modulo $9$. There are $54$ units in $\Z[\omega]/(9)$, and a direct enumeration shows that the $27$ products $\omega^b2^c(1+3\omega)^d$ with $b,c,d\in\{0,1,2\}$ are pairwise distinct modulo $9$, also after multiplication by $-1=(-1)^3$. Hence they represent all $27$ classes of units modulo cubes. Since $\pi^3$ is a cube times a unit, the exponent of $\pi$ is the valuation modulo $3$, and the first statement follows.

By (H1), it remains to compute $\theta_v$ on the $16$ ordered pairs of the elements $\pi$, $\omega$, $2$ and $1+3\omega$. Their norms are $3$, $1$, $4$ and $7$, hence, for each such pair, all the symbols at the places not above $2$, $3$ and $7$ vanish by (H2), and, by (H3), $\theta_v$ at the place above $3$ is minus the sum of the values at the place above $2$ and at the two places above $7$. These places correspond to $\bar\omega=2$ and $\bar\omega=4$ in $\F_7$. The classes $(e,\chi)$ of the four elements at these places are
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 & 2 & \bar\omega=2 & \bar\omega=4\\\hline
\pi & (0,2) & (0,0) & (0,2)\\
\omega & (0,1) & (0,2) & (0,2)\\
2 & (1,0) & (0,2) & (0,1)\\
1+3\omega & (0,2) & (1,0) & (0,0)
\end{array}
\]
where, at the places above $7$, $\chi(u)$ is defined by $\bar u^2=\bar\omega^{\chi(u)}$ in $\F_7$, and $7$ is a uniformizer. Formula (H2) and the sum over these three places give $M_3$.
\end{proof}

We write $\theta_3$ for the form of Lemma~\ref{lem:cM3}. Since $3=-\omega^2\pi^2$ and $4=2^2$, we have $\mathrm{cl}_3(3)=(2,2,0,0)$ and $\mathrm{cl}_3(4)=(0,0,2,0)$.

\paragraph{Residue classes.} The local computations use the following description of the classes of the values of a polynomial on a residue class.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cresidue}
Let $p$ be a prime, $k\geq1$, and let $z\in\Z[\omega]$ be non-zero with $z\equiv\zeta\pmod{p^k}$, where $\zeta=\langle A,B\rangle$ with $0\leq A,B<p^k$. Then $\mathrm{cl}_p(z)\in b+D$ for the vector $b$ and the subspace $D$ given as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $p\equiv2\pmod3$. If $\zeta\neq0$, then $b=\mathrm{cl}_p(\zeta)$ and $D=0$. If $\zeta=0$, then $b=0$, and $D$ is $\F_3^2$, or the span of $(1,0)$ if $z\in\Z$.
\item[(ii)] $p\equiv1\pmod3$. For each root $r$, let $\rho$ be the root of $t^2+t+1$ modulo $p^k$ congruent to $r$. If $A+B\rho\not\equiv0\pmod{p^k}$, the two coordinates of $\mathrm{cl}_{v_r}(z)$ are those of $\mathrm{cl}_{v_r}(\zeta)$; otherwise these two coordinates are arbitrary.
\item[(iii)] $p=3$, $z\notin\Z$. If $\zeta\neq0$, let $a=v(\zeta)<2k$ be its valuation at $\pi$ and $m=2k-a$. Then $b=\mathrm{cl}_3(\zeta)$, and $D=0$ if $m\geq4$, while $D=D_m$ if $m\leq3$, where $D_1$ is spanned by $(0,1,0,0)$, $(0,0,1,0)$ and $(0,0,0,1)$, $D_2$ by $(0,0,1,0)$ and $(0,0,0,1)$, and $D_3$ by $(0,0,1,1)$. If $\zeta=0$, then $b=0$ and $D=\F_3^4$.
\item[(iv)] $p=3$, $z\in\Z$, so that $B=0$. If $3^k\nmid A$, let $j=v_3(A)$. Then $b=\mathrm{cl}_3(A)$ and $D=0$ if $k-j\geq2$, while $b=j\,\mathrm{cl}_3(3)$ and $D$ is spanned by $\mathrm{cl}_3(4)$ if $k-j=1$. If $3^k\mid A$, then $b=0$ and $D$ is spanned by $\mathrm{cl}_3(3)$ and $\mathrm{cl}_3(4)$.
\end{itemize}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) If $\zeta\neq0$, then $e=v(z)=v(\zeta)<k$ and $z/p^e\equiv\zeta/p^e\pmod p$, so that $\chi$ is determined. If $z\in\Z$, its unit part is a rational integer, and $\chi=0$. (ii) At $v_r$, $z$ and $\zeta$ are the $p$-adic numbers $A'+B'\omega$ and $A+B\omega$ with $\omega\equiv\rho\pmod{p^k}$, and the argument of (i) applies. (iii) If $\zeta\neq0$, then $v(z)=a$, and $z/\pi^a\equiv\zeta/\pi^a\pmod{\pi^m}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:cM3}, the class of a unit depends only on its residue modulo $9=\omega\pi^4$; the units congruent to $1$ modulo $\pi^m$ form a subgroup, and a direct enumeration of their residues modulo $9$ shows that their classes form $D_m$. (iv) If $3^k\nmid A$, then $z=3^ju$ with a rational unit $u\equiv A/3^j\pmod{3^{k-j}}$. The class of a rational unit depends only on its residue modulo $9$, the units $\pm1$ are cubes, and the rational units congruent to $1$ modulo $3$ are $4^s$ times a unit congruent to $1$ modulo $9$; hence the classes are as stated, and if $3^k\mid A$ the valuation $j$ is also unknown.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cenvelope}
Let $p$ be a prime, and consider vertices $i$ with vectors $b_i$, subspaces $D_i$ and vectors $\gamma_i$, and a set of edges $(i,j)$ with $i<j$ and weights $w_{ij}\in\{1,2\}$. Put $\rho_{ij}=w_{ij}$ and $\rho_{ji}=-w_{ij}$ for the edges, and $\sigma_i=\gamma_i+\sum_j\rho_{ij}b_j$. Suppose that $\theta_p(d,\sigma_i)=0$ for every $i$ and every $d\in D_i$, and that $\theta_p(d,d')=0$ for every edge $(i,j)$ and all $d\in D_i$, $d'\in D_j$. Then
\[
\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(c_i,c_j)+\sum_i\theta_p(c_i,\gamma_i)=\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(b_i,b_j)+\sum_i\theta_p(b_i,\gamma_i)
\]
whenever $c_i\in b_i+D_i$ for every $i$. By bilinearity, it suffices to check the hypotheses for $d$ and $d'$ in spanning sets of $D_i$ and $D_j$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $c_i=b_i+d_i$ with $d_i\in D_i$. As $\theta_p$ is bilinear and $\theta_p(u,v)=-\theta_p(v,u)$, the left side equals the right side plus $\sum_i\theta_p(d_i,\sigma_i)+\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(d_i,d_j)$, and both sums vanish.
\end{proof}

\section{The quadratic certificate}

Put $F(x,y)=2x^{4}+x+y^{3}+y+3$, so that \eqref{eq:main} is the equation $F(x,y)=0$. The vertices are the polynomials
\begin{align*}
H_{0}&=-y-1,\\
H_{1}&=x^{2}+x+1,\\
H_{2}&=3x^{2}+3xy+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{3}&=x^{2}+xy+x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{4}&=4x^{2}+2xy+4x+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{5}&=x^{2}-xy-2x+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{6}&=9x^{2}-9xy+3y^{2}+3y+3,\\
H_{7}&=x^{2}-2x-y-1,\\
H_{8}&=x-1,\\
H_{9}&=xy+x,\\
H_{10}&=x^{2}-xy+y^{2},\\
H_{11}&=-xy+2x-y,\\
H_{12}&=x^{2}-xy-x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{13}&=-y+1,\\
H_{14}&=xy+2x+2,\\
H_{15}&=x^{2}-xy+3x+y^{2}+3,\\
H_{16}&=y+1,\\
H_{17}&=-2xy+3x-y,\\
H_{18}&=9x^{2}+3y^{2},\\
H_{19}&=-2y,\\
H_{20}&=-x^{3}-x^{2}-y,\\
H_{21}&=x^{4}+x^{2}y+y^{2},\\
H_{22}&=x^{3}+x^{2}+x-y,\\
H_{23}&=x^{4}-x^{2}y+x^{2}+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{24}&=x^{2}+1,\\
H_{25}&=x^{3}+x^{2}-y,\\
H_{26}&=x^{4}+x^{3}-x^{2}y-2xy+x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{27}&=x^{2}-1,\\
H_{28}&=-x^{3}-x^{2}-2x-y-1,\\
H_{29}&=x^{4}+x^{3}-x^{2}y+2x^{2}+xy+2x+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{30}&=-x^{2}-x-1,\\
H_{31}&=x^{3}-x-y-2,\\
H_{32}&=x^{4}+x^{2}y+y^{2}+3y+3,\\
H_{33}&=x^{3}+x^{2}+x-y-1,\\
H_{34}&=x^{4}-x^{2}y+y^{2}+3y+3,\\
H_{35}&=-xy+x-y-1,\\
H_{36}&=-y,\\
H_{37}&=-x-y,\\
H_{38}&=y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{39}&=-1,\\
H_{40}&=x-y+1,\\
H_{41}&=x+1,\\
H_{42}&=x^{2}+xy-2x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{43}&=x^{2}+xy-x+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{44}&=x^{2}-x+1,\\
H_{45}&=x+y+1,\\
H_{46}&=x+y-1,\\
H_{47}&=xy+2x-y+1,\\
H_{48}&=2x+y+1,\\
H_{49}&=-xy+2x+y+1,\\
H_{50}&=-2xy+3x+y,\\
H_{51}&=x^{3}-y+1,\\
H_{52}&=-x^{3}-x-y-1,\\
H_{53}&=x^{4}+x^{3}-x^{2}y+x^{2}+xy+3x+y^{2}+3y+3,\\
H_{54}&=x-y,\\
H_{55}&=-x^{2}-x-y+1,\\

For a vertex $i$, we write $j\sim i$ if $\{i,j\}$ is an edge.
For every non-constant vertex $i$, Table~\ref{tab:star} gives a positive integer $m_{i}$ and polynomials $W_{i}$, $A_{i}$ and $B_{i}$ with integer coefficients such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:star}
m_i^2\prod_{j\sim i}H_j-W_i^2=A_iF+B_iH_i.
\end{equation}
For every edge $\{i,j\}$ between two non-constant vertices, Table~\ref{tab:sep} gives a positive integer $k_{ij}$ and polynomials $\alpha_{ij}$, $\beta_{ij}$ and $\gamma_{ij}$ with integer coefficients such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sep}
\alpha_{ij}F+\beta_{ij}H_i+\gamma_{ij}H_j=k_{ij}.
\end{equation}
These identities can be checked by expanding both sides.

\section{The cubic certificates}

There are 2 certificates. The $g$-th certificate consists of polynomials $J^{(g)}_i$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ and constants $c^{(g)}_i\in\Z[\omega]$, listed in Table~\ref{tab:cvert1} and Table~\ref{tab:cvert2}, and of \emph{edges} $(i,j)$ with $i<j$ and weights $w^{(g)}_{ij}\in\{1,2\}$. For an edge we put $\rho^{(g)}_{ij}=w^{(g)}_{ij}$ and $\rho^{(g)}_{ji}=-w^{(g)}_{ij}\bmod3$, and $\rho^{(g)}_{ij}=0$ otherwise. In the rest of the proof we usually suppress the index $g$.

Let $(\xi,\eta)$ denote $(x,y)$ or $(y,x)$. We call a vertex $i$ \emph{linear} if $J_i=\varepsilon_i(\lambda_i\eta-s_i(\xi))$ for a unit $\varepsilon_i$ of $\Z[\omega]$, an element $\lambda_i\in\Z[\omega]$ whose norm is a power of $3$, and a polynomial $s_i$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$; the last column of Table~\ref{tab:cvert1} and its analogues gives $\lambda_i\eta=s_i(\xi)$ for the linear vertices, where $\lambda_i=1$ if no factor is shown. For a linear vertex $i$, we put $f_i(t)=\lambda_i^{d}F$ and $g_{ij}(t)=\lambda_i^{d_j}J_j$ evaluated at $\xi=t$, $\eta=s_i(t)/\lambda_i$, where $d$ and $d_j$ are the degrees of $F$ and $J_j$ in $\eta$; these are polynomials in $t$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$.
For every linear vertex $i$, Table~\ref{tab:cstar} gives an integer $D_i\geq1$ and a polynomial $w_i(t)$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cstarlin}
D_i^3\,\lambda_i^{e_i}c_i\prod_jg_{ij}^{\rho_{ij}}-w_i^3=f_iQ_i
\end{equation}
for a polynomial $Q_i(t)$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$, where $\rho_{ij}\in\{0,1,2\}$; the table also gives an integer $e_i\geq0$ with $e_i+\sum_j\rho_{ij}d_j\equiv0\pmod3$, and $e_i=0$ if $\lambda_i=1$; $Q_i$ is the quotient of the division of the left side by $f_i$, and the remainder is zero.
For every other vertex $i$, Table~\ref{tab:cstar} gives an integer $D_i\geq1$ and polynomials $T_i$, $U_i$ and $V_i$ in $x$ and $y$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cstar}
D_i^3\,c_i\prod_jJ_j^{\rho_{ij}}-T_i^3=U_iF+V_iJ_i.
\end{equation}
For the edges $(i,j)$ listed in Table~\ref{tab:csep}, the table gives a linear endpoint $k\in\{i,j\}$ and the factorization of the norm of the resultant $R_{ij}=\operatorname{Res}_t(f_k,g_{kl})$, where $\{k,l\}=\{i,j\}$; it is non-zero.
These identities can be checked by expanding both sides and by polynomial division, using $\omega^2=-1-\omega$. They hold for each certificate, with its own data.

\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}}

Suppose that $(x,y)\in\Z^2$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:main}, so that $F(x,y)=0$; from now on the polynomials denote their values at $(x,y)$.
\paragraph{Step 1: the values are non-zero.} For $i\in\{21,\allowbreak 29,\allowbreak 47,\allowbreak 49,\allowbreak 53,\allowbreak 69,\allowbreak 74,\allowbreak 77,\allowbreak 80,\allowbreak 81,\allowbreak 82,\allowbreak 103,\allowbreak 120,\allowbreak 138,\allowbreak 152,\allowbreak 155,\allowbreak 161,\allowbreak 163,\allowbreak 186,\allowbreak 188,\allowbreak 189,\allowbreak 190\}$, checking the $2^2$ residue pairs modulo $2$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $2$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{14,\allowbreak 26,\allowbreak 51,\allowbreak 65,\allowbreak 78,\allowbreak 79,\allowbreak 85,\allowbreak 122,\allowbreak 133,\allowbreak 135,\allowbreak 143,\allowbreak 164,\allowbreak 168\}$, checking the $3^2$ residue pairs modulo $3$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $3$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{66,\allowbreak 71,\allowbreak 145,\allowbreak 147\}$, checking the $4^2$ residue pairs modulo $4$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $4$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{8,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 27,\allowbreak 28,\allowbreak 32,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 35,\allowbreak 41,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 57,\allowbreak 60,\allowbreak 63,\allowbreak 64,\allowbreak 67,\allowbreak 88,\allowbreak 95,\allowbreak 100,\allowbreak 101,\allowbreak 106,\allowbreak 107,\allowbreak 108,\allowbreak 109,\allowbreak 110,\allowbreak 114,\allowbreak 117,\allowbreak 119,\allowbreak 123,\allowbreak 125,\allowbreak 128,\allowbreak 131,\allowbreak 137,\allowbreak 144,\allowbreak 153,\allowbreak 165,\allowbreak 166,\allowbreak 167,\allowbreak 169,\allowbreak 170,\allowbreak 171,\allowbreak 174,\allowbreak 177,\allowbreak 181,\allowbreak 183,\allowbreak 216\}$, checking the $5^2$ residue pairs modulo $5$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $5$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{52,\allowbreak 116,\allowbreak 154,\allowbreak 158,\allowbreak 162,\allowbreak 173,\allowbreak 184\}$, checking the $7^2$ residue pairs modulo $7$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $7$, so $H_i\neq0$. Checking the $8^2$ residue pairs modulo $8$, we find that $F$ and $H_{23}$ have no common zero modulo $8$, so $H_{23}\neq0$. Checking the $9^2$ residue pairs modulo $9$, we find that $F$ and $H_{140}$ have no common zero modulo $9$, so $H_{140}\neq0$. For $i\in\{31,\allowbreak 86,\allowbreak 98,\allowbreak 130,\allowbreak 134,\allowbreak 149,\allowbreak 150,\allowbreak 160,\allowbreak 172,\allowbreak 182\}$, checking the $11^2$ residue pairs modulo $11$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $11$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{25,\allowbreak 124,\allowbreak 141,\allowbreak 178\}$, checking the $13^2$ residue pairs modulo $13$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $13$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{9,\allowbreak 22,\allowbreak 90,\allowbreak 126,\allowbreak 159,\allowbreak 175\}$, checking the $17^2$ residue pairs modulo $17$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $17$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{142,\allowbreak 148,\allowbreak 157\}$, checking the $19^2$ residue pairs modulo $19$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $19$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{115,\allowbreak 129\}$, checking the $23^2$ residue pairs modulo $23$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $23$, so $H_i\neq0$. Checking the $25^2$ residue pairs modulo $25$, we find that $F$ and $H_{121}$ have no common zero modulo $25$, so $H_{121}\neq0$. Checking the $41^2$ residue pairs modulo $41$, we find that $F$ and $H_{91}$ have no common zero modulo $41$, so $H_{91}\neq0$. Checking the $49^2$ residue pairs modulo $49$, we find that $F$ and $H_{99}$ have no common zero modulo $49$, so $H_{99}\neq0$. The other non-constant $H_i$ are non-zero by Step~2. For $i\in\{1,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 6,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 8,\allowbreak 9,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 14,\allowbreak 15,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 27,\allowbreak 29,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 39,\allowbreak 42,\allowbreak 46,\allowbreak 47,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 51,\allowbreak 60,\allowbreak 62,\allowbreak 65,\allowbreak 69,\allowbreak 70\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $2$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{2}$, as one checks for the $2^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{2,\allowbreak 13,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 18,\allowbreak 19,\allowbreak 28,\allowbreak 38,\allowbreak 45,\allowbreak 52,\allowbreak 54,\allowbreak 58,\allowbreak 59,\allowbreak 66,\allowbreak 67,\allowbreak 68\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $3$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{3}$, as one checks for the $3^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{16,\allowbreak 21,\allowbreak 22,\allowbreak 23,\allowbreak 24,\allowbreak 25,\allowbreak 26,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 35,\allowbreak 36,\allowbreak 41,\allowbreak 53,\allowbreak 55,\allowbreak 56\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $4$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{4}$, as one checks for the $4^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{0,\allowbreak 4,\allowbreak 12,\allowbreak 32,\allowbreak 40,\allowbreak 43,\allowbreak 48,\allowbreak 49,\allowbreak 61,\allowbreak 63,\allowbreak 71\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $5$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{5}$, as one checks for the $5^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{31,\allowbreak 37,\allowbreak 64\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $11$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{11}$, as one checks for the $11^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{57\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $13$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{13}$, as one checks for the $13^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{44\}$ in the first certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $17$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{17}$, as one checks for the $17^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{1,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 6,\allowbreak 8,\allowbreak 9,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 14,\allowbreak 16,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 19,\allowbreak 25,\allowbreak 27,\allowbreak 28,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 31,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 40,\allowbreak 43,\allowbreak 44,\allowbreak 47,\allowbreak 48,\allowbreak 53,\allowbreak 60,\allowbreak 63,\allowbreak 64,\allowbreak 65,\allowbreak 66\}$ in the second certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $2$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{2}$, as one checks for the $2^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{2,\allowbreak 13,\allowbreak 18,\allowbreak 26,\allowbreak 29,\allowbreak 38,\allowbreak 42,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 51,\allowbreak 54,\allowbreak 56,\allowbreak 62\}$ in the second certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $3$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{3}$, as one checks for the $3^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{12,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 21,\allowbreak 22,\allowbreak 23,\allowbreak 24,\allowbreak 35,\allowbreak 36,\allowbreak 39,\allowbreak 55,\allowbreak 58,\allowbreak 59,\allowbreak 61,\allowbreak 67\}$ in the second certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $4$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{4}$, as one checks for the $4^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{0,\allowbreak 4,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 15,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 45,\allowbreak 46,\allowbreak 49,\allowbreak 52,\allowbreak 57,\allowbreak 68\}$ in the second certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $5$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{5}$, as one checks for the $5^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{32,\allowbreak 37\}$ in the second certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $11$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{11}$, as one checks for the $11^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{41\}$ in the second certificate, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $17$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{17}$, as one checks for the $17^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. Also, all $c_i$ are non-zero.

\paragraph{Step 2: signs.} \emph{The sign of $H_{0}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign0} gives $Q_{0}H_{0}=M_{0}$, and $M_{0}$ and $E_{0}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{0}>0$ and $E_{0}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{0}$ and $E_{0}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{0}>0$ and $E_{0}>0$. It remains to consider $x=0$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{0}>0$ and $E_{0}>0$. Hence $M_{0}>0$ and $E_{0}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign0b} gives $4Q_{0}\geq E_{0}>0$, and $Q_{0}H_{0}=M_{0}>0$ gives $H_{0}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{1}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign1}, $H_{1}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{2}$.} If $H_{2}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign2} gives $(3x+2y+1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x-1)^2\leq 0$, so $|3x+2y+1|\leq0$ and $|x-1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(1,-2)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{2}\leq0$. Hence $H_{2}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{3}$.} If $H_{3}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign3} gives $(x+2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{3}\leq0$. Hence $H_{3}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{4}$.} If $H_{4}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign4} gives $(2x+2y+1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(2x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|2x+2y+1|\leq0$ and $|2x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that no integer point $(x,y)$ satisfies both inequalities. Hence $H_{4}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{5}$.} If $H_{5}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign5} gives $(x-2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x-1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x-2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x-1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(1,0)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{5}\leq0$. Hence $H_{5}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{6}$.} If $H_{6}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign6} gives $3(3x-2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $9(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|3x-2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,-2)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{6}\leq0$. Hence $H_{6}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{7}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign7} gives $Q_{7}H_{7}=M_{7}$, and $M_{7}$ and $E_{7}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{7}>0$ and $E_{7}>0$. If $x\geq3$, write $x=3+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{7}$ and $E_{7}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{7}>0$ and $E_{7}>0$. It remains to consider $0\leq x\leq2$. For $x=0$ and $x=2$ we have $M_{7}>0$ and $E_{7}>0$. For $x=1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{7}>0$ and $E_{7}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign7b} gives $4Q_{7}\geq E_{7}>0$, and $Q_{7}H_{7}=M_{7}>0$ gives $H_{7}>0$.

Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer points satisfying both inequalities are $(-1,-1)$, $(-1,0)$, $(-1,1)$, $(-1,2)$, $(0,-1)$, $(0,0)$, $(0,1)$, $(1,-2)$, $(1,-1)$, $(1,0)$, $(1,1)$, $(2,-2)$, $(2,-1)$, $(2,0)$, and none of them is a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{94}\leq0$. Hence $H_{94}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{96}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{96})\leq0$. Then \eqref{eq:sign96} gives $(x+y-3)(y+2)<0$, so the two factors have opposite signs. As these factors are integers, this means that $x+y\geq4$ and $y\leq-3$, or $x+y\leq2$ and $y\geq-1$. In the first case, write $x+y=4+X$ and $y=-3-Y$ with $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=X+Y+7$ and $y=-Y-3$. Then $F(x,y)=G_{96,1}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{96,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{4}+8X^{3}Y+56X^{3}+12X^{2}Y^{2}+168X^{2}Y+588X^{2}+8XY^{3}\\
&\quad+168XY^{2}+1176XY+2745X+2Y^{4}+55Y^{3}+579Y^{2}+2717Y+4782.
\end{align*}
All coefficients of $G_{96,1}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{96,1}(X,Y)\neq0$. In the second case, write $x+y=2-X$ and $y=-1+Y$ with $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=-X-Y+3$ and $y=Y-1$. Then $F(x,y)=G_{96,2}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{96,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{4}+8X^{3}Y-24X^{3}+12X^{2}Y^{2}-72X^{2}Y+108X^{2}+8XY^{3}\\
&\quad-72XY^{2}+216XY-217X+2Y^{4}-23Y^{3}+105Y^{2}-213Y+166.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{96,2}(4+X,4+Y)$, and, for $0\leq i<4$, $G_{96,2}(i,4+Y)$ and $G_{96,2}(4+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{96,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $0\leq i,j<4$. Hence $G_{96,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{96})>0$. Hence $H_{96}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{97}$.} If $H_{97}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign97} gives $3(y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $9x^2\leq 0$, so $|y-1|\leq0$ and $|x|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(0,1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{97}\leq0$. Hence $H_{97}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{102}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign102} gives $Q_{102}(-H_{102})=M_{102}$, and $M_{102}$ and $E_{102}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{102}$ and $E_{102}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$. It remains to consider $x=0$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$. Hence $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign102b} gives $4Q_{102}\geq E_{102}>0$, and $Q_{102}(-H_{102})=M_{102}>0$ gives $(-H_{102})>0$. Hence $H_{102}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{104}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{104})\leq0$. Then \eqref{eq:sign104} gives $(2y+1)(-4x+2y-3)<0$, so the two factors have opposite signs. As these factors are integers, this means that $y\geq0$ and $-2x+y\leq1$, or $y\leq-1$ and $-2x+y\geq2$. In the first case, write $y=X$ and $-2x+y=-Y+1$ with integers $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=\tfrac{X+Y-1}{2}$ and $y=X$. Then $8F(x,y)=G_{104,1}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{104,1}(X,Y)&=X^{4}+4X^{3}Y+4X^{3}+6X^{2}Y^{2}-12X^{2}Y+6X^{2}+4XY^{3}\\
&\quad-12XY^{2}+12XY+8X+Y^{4}-4Y^{3}+6Y^{2}+21.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{104,1}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{104,1}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{104,1}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{104,1}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Hence $G_{104,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In the second case, write $y=-X-1$ and $-2x+y=Y+2$ with integers $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=\tfrac{-X-Y-3}{2}$ and $y=-X-1$. Then $8F(x,y)=G_{104,2}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{104,2}(X,Y)&=X^{4}+4X^{3}Y+4X^{3}+6X^{2}Y^{2}+36X^{2}Y+30X^{2}+4XY^{3}\\
&\quad+36XY^{2}+108XY+72X+Y^{4}+12Y^{3}+54Y^{2}+104Y+77.
\end{align*}
All coefficients of $G_{104,2}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{104,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{104})>0$. Hence $H_{104}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{105}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign105} gives $Q_{105}H_{105}=M_{105}$, and $M_{105}$ and $E_{105}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{105}>0$ and $E_{105}>0$. \emph{The sign of $H_{113}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{113})\leq0$, and put $Y=H_{113}$, so that $Y\geq0$. Since $(-H_{113})=-x+y^{2}+2y$, we have $x=y^{2}+2y+Y$. If $y\geq0$, put $X=y$; if $y\leq-1$, put $X=-1-y$. In both cases $X\geq0$, and substituting into $F$ gives $F(x,y)=G_{113,1}(X,Y)$ in the first case and $F(x,y)=G_{113,2}(X,Y)$ in the second, where

All coefficients of $G_{113,1}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{113,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. All coefficients of $G_{132,2}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{132,2}(X,Y)\neq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{132}>0$.

If $y\geq0$, put $X=y$; if $y\leq-1$, put $X=-1-y$. In both cases $X\geq0$, and substituting into $F$ gives $F(x,y)=G_{139,1}(X,Y)$ in the first case and $F(x,y)=G_{139,2}(X,Y)$ in the second, where
\begin{align*}
G_{139,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+8X^{7}+8X^{6}Y+12X^{6}+24X^{5}Y+8X^{5}+12X^{4}Y^{2}\\

Expanding $G_{139,1}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{139,1}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{139,1}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{139,1}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Expanding $G_{139,2}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{139,2}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{139,2}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{139,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. \emph{The sign of $H_{151}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{151})\leq0$. Then \eqref{eq:sign151} gives $(2y-3)(-x+y+1)<0$, so the two factors have opposite signs. All coefficients of $G_{151,1}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{151,1}(X,Y)\neq0$. In the second case, write $y=1-X$ and $-x+y=Y$ with $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=-X-Y+1$ and $y=-X+1$. Then $F(x,y)=G_{151,2}(X,Y)$, where

G_{151,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{4}+8X^{3}Y-9X^{3}+12X^{2}Y^{2}-24X^{2}Y+15X^{2}+8XY^{3}\\
&\quad-24XY^{2}+24XY-13X+2Y^{4}-8Y^{3}+12Y^{2}-9Y+8.

Expanding $G_{151,2}(2+X,2+Y)$, and, for $0\leq i<2$, $G_{151,2}(i,2+Y)$ and $G_{151,2}(2+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{151,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $0\leq i,j<2$. \emph{The sign of $H_{156}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign156} gives $Q_{156}(-H_{156})=M_{156}$, and $M_{156}$ and $E_{156}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{156}>0$ and $E_{156}>0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{156}$ and $E_{156}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{156}>0$ and $E_{156}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq0$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. \emph{The sign of $H_{179}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{179})\leq0$. Then \eqref{eq:sign179} gives $(y+3)(-x+y-2)<0$, so the two factors have opposite signs. \begin{align*}
G_{180,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+8X^{6}Y-8X^{6}+12X^{4}Y^{2}-24X^{4}Y+12X^{4}+X^{3}\\
&\quad+8X^{2}Y^{3}-24X^{2}Y^{2}+24X^{2}Y-7X^{2}+X+2Y^{4}-8Y^{3}\\
&\quad+12Y^{2}-7Y+4,\\
G_{180,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+16X^{7}+8X^{6}Y+48X^{6}+48X^{5}Y+64X^{5}+12X^{4}Y^{2}\\
&\quad+96X^{4}Y+32X^{4}+48X^{3}Y^{2}+64X^{3}Y-X^{3}+8X^{2}Y^{3}\\
&\quad+48X^{2}Y^{2}-2X^{2}+16XY^{3}-2X+2Y^{4}+Y+1.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{180,1}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{180,1}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{180,1}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{180,1}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Hence $G_{180,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. Expanding $G_{180,2}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{180,2}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{180,2}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{180,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Hence $G_{180,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{180}>0$.

For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{219}>0$ and $E_{219}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign219b} gives $16Q_{219}\geq E_{219}>0$, and $Q_{219}H_{219}=M_{219}>0$ gives $H_{219}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{220}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign220} gives $Q_{220}H_{220}=M_{220}$, and $M_{220}$ and $E_{220}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{220}>0$ and $E_{220}>0$. If $x\geq2$, write $x=2+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{220}$ and $E_{220}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{220}>0$ and $E_{220}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq1$. For $x=-1$ and $x=0$ we have $M_{220}>0$ and $E_{220}>0$. For $x=1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{220}>0$ and $E_{220}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign220b} gives $100Q_{220}\geq E_{220}>0$, and $Q_{220}H_{220}=M_{220}>0$ gives $H_{220}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{221}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign221} gives $Q_{221}(-H_{221})=M_{221}$, and $M_{221}$ and $E_{221}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{221}>0$ and $E_{221}>0$. If $x\geq6$, write $x=6+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-3$, write $x=-3-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{221}$ and $E_{221}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{221}>0$ and $E_{221}>0$. It remains to consider $-2\leq x\leq5$. For $x\in\{-2,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak 2,\allowbreak 4\}$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=-1$ and $x=1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=5$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{11}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 11 residues of $y$ modulo $11$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=3$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{47}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 47 residues of $y$ modulo $47$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{221}>0$ and $E_{221}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign221b} gives $16Q_{221}\geq E_{221}>0$, and $Q_{221}(-H_{221})=M_{221}>0$ gives $(-H_{221})>0$. Hence $H_{221}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{222}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign222} gives $Q_{222}H_{222}=M_{222}$, and $M_{222}$ and $E_{222}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{222}>0$ and $E_{222}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-7$, write $x=-7-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{222}$ and $E_{222}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{222}>0$ and $E_{222}>0$. It remains to consider $-6\leq x\leq0$. For $x\in\{-6,\allowbreak -4,\allowbreak -2,\allowbreak 0\}$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-3$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{7}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 7 residues of $y$ modulo $7$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-5$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{11}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 11 residues of $y$ modulo $11$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{222}>0$ and $E_{222}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign222b} gives $4Q_{222}\geq E_{222}>0$, and $Q_{222}H_{222}=M_{222}>0$ gives $H_{222}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{223}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign223} gives $Q_{223}(-H_{223})=M_{223}$, and $M_{223}$ and $E_{223}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{223}>0$ and $E_{223}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{223}$ and $E_{223}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{223}>0$ and $E_{223}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$ and $x=0$ we have $M_{223}>0$ and $E_{223}>0$. Hence $M_{223}>0$ and $E_{223}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign223b} gives $16Q_{223}\geq E_{223}>0$, and $Q_{223}(-H_{223})=M_{223}>0$ gives $(-H_{223})>0$. Hence $H_{223}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{224}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign224} gives $Q_{224}H_{224}=M_{224}$, and $M_{224}$ and $E_{224}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{224}>0$ and $E_{224}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-3$, write $x=-3-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{224}$ and $E_{224}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{224}>0$ and $E_{224}>0$. It remains to consider $-2\leq x\leq0$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{224}>0$ and $E_{224}>0$. For $x=-2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{224}>0$ and $E_{224}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign224b} gives $16Q_{224}\geq E_{224}>0$, and $Q_{224}H_{224}=M_{224}>0$ gives $H_{224}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{225}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign225} gives $Q_{225}H_{225}=M_{225}$, and $M_{225}$ and $E_{225}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{225}>0$ and $E_{225}>0$. If $x\geq6$, write $x=6+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{225}$ and $E_{225}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{225}>0$ and $E_{225}>0$. It remains to consider $0\leq x\leq5$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{225}>0$ and $E_{225}>0$. For $x=2$ and $x=4$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=5$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{11}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 11 residues of $y$ modulo $11$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=3$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{47}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 47 residues of $y$ modulo $47$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{225}>0$ and $E_{225}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign225b} gives $4Q_{225}\geq E_{225}>0$, and $Q_{225}H_{225}=M_{225}>0$ gives $H_{225}>0$.

For a place $v$, put $B_v=\prod_{\{i,j\}}(H_i,H_j)_v$, the product over the edges. By Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(i), $(H_i,H_j)_\infty=-1$ exactly when $H_i<0$ and $H_j<0$. Here $H_i>0$ for $i\in\{0,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 2,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 4,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 6,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 12,\allowbreak 13,\allowbreak 15,\allowbreak 18,\allowbreak 19,\allowbreak 21,\allowbreak 23,\allowbreak 24,\allowbreak 26,\allowbreak 29,\allowbreak 32,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 36,\allowbreak 37,\allowbreak 38,\allowbreak 40,\allowbreak 42,\allowbreak 43,\allowbreak 44,\allowbreak 53,\allowbreak 54,\allowbreak 59,\allowbreak 61,\allowbreak 62,\allowbreak 68,\allowbreak 70,\allowbreak 72,\allowbreak 73,\allowbreak 75,\allowbreak 76,\allowbreak 80,\allowbreak 83,\allowbreak 84,\allowbreak 87,\allowbreak 92,\allowbreak 93,\allowbreak 94,\allowbreak 97,\allowbreak 105,\allowbreak 111,\allowbreak 118,\allowbreak 120,\allowbreak 123,\allowbreak 127,\allowbreak 132,\allowbreak 139,\allowbreak 176,\allowbreak 180,\allowbreak 185,\allowbreak 187,\allowbreak 191,\allowbreak 193,\allowbreak 194,\allowbreak 195,\allowbreak 196,\allowbreak 198,\allowbreak 199,\allowbreak 200,\allowbreak 202,\allowbreak 204,\allowbreak 205,\allowbreak 207,\allowbreak 208,\allowbreak 210,\allowbreak 211,\allowbreak 212,\allowbreak 213,\allowbreak 214,\allowbreak 218,\allowbreak 219,\allowbreak 220,\allowbreak 222,\allowbreak 224,\allowbreak 225,\allowbreak 226\}$ and $H_i<0$ for $i\in\{16,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 39,\allowbreak 45,\allowbreak 46,\allowbreak 48,\allowbreak 55,\allowbreak 56,\allowbreak 58,\allowbreak 89,\allowbreak 96,\allowbreak 102,\allowbreak 104,\allowbreak 112,\allowbreak 113,\allowbreak 136,\allowbreak 146,\allowbreak 151,\allowbreak 156,\allowbreak 174,\allowbreak 179,\allowbreak 192,\allowbreak 197,\allowbreak 201,\allowbreak 203,\allowbreak 206,\allowbreak 209,\allowbreak 215,\allowbreak 217,\allowbreak 221,\allowbreak 223\}$. For $i\in\{8,\allowbreak 9,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 14,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 22,\allowbreak 25,\allowbreak 27,\allowbreak 28,\allowbreak 31,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 35,\allowbreak 41,\allowbreak 47,\allowbreak 49,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 51,\allowbreak 52,\allowbreak 57,\allowbreak 60,\allowbreak 63,\allowbreak 64,\allowbreak 65,\allowbreak 66,\allowbreak 67,\allowbreak 69,\allowbreak 71,\allowbreak 74,\allowbreak 77,\allowbreak 78,\allowbreak 79,\allowbreak 81,\allowbreak 82,\allowbreak 85,\allowbreak 86,\allowbreak 88,\allowbreak 90,\allowbreak 91,\allowbreak 95,\allowbreak 98,\allowbreak 99,\allowbreak 100,\allowbreak 101,\allowbreak 103,\allowbreak 106,\allowbreak 107,\allowbreak 108,\allowbreak 109,\allowbreak 110,\allowbreak 114,\allowbreak 115,\allowbreak 116,\allowbreak 117,\allowbreak 119,\allowbreak 121,\allowbreak 122,\allowbreak 124,\allowbreak 125,\allowbreak 126,\allowbreak 128,\allowbreak 129,\allowbreak 130,\allowbreak 131,\allowbreak 133,\allowbreak 134,\allowbreak 135,\allowbreak 137,\allowbreak 138,\allowbreak 140,\allowbreak 141,\allowbreak 142,\allowbreak 143,\allowbreak 144,\allowbreak 145,\allowbreak 147,\allowbreak 148,\allowbreak 149,\allowbreak 150,\allowbreak 152,\allowbreak 153,\allowbreak 154,\allowbreak 155,\allowbreak 157,\allowbreak 158,\allowbreak 159,\allowbreak 160,\allowbreak 161,\allowbreak 162,\allowbreak 163,\allowbreak 164,\allowbreak 165,\allowbreak 166,\allowbreak 167,\allowbreak 168,\allowbreak 169,\allowbreak 170,\allowbreak 171,\allowbreak 172,\allowbreak 173,\allowbreak 175,\allowbreak 177,\allowbreak 178,\allowbreak 181,\allowbreak 182,\allowbreak 183,\allowbreak 184,\allowbreak 186,\allowbreak 188,\allowbreak 189,\allowbreak 190,\allowbreak 216\}$, the sign of $H_i$ is not needed: $H_j>0$ for every edge $\{i,j\}$, so $(H_i,H_j)_\infty=1$. Counting the edges whose two values are negative, we find $B_\infty=1$.

\paragraph{Step 3: the quadratic graphs at the odd primes outside $S$.} Let $S=\{2,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 13,\allowbreak 19,\allowbreak 31,\allowbreak 37,\allowbreak 43\}$. It contains $2$ and the primes dividing the $m_{i}$, the $k_{ij}$ and the constant vertices. Let $p\notin S$ be an odd prime; we show that $B_p=1$. For an edge $\{i,j\}$ such that $p$ divides neither $H_i$ nor $H_j$, $(H_i,H_j)_p=1$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(ii). Now let $i$ be a vertex with $p\mid H_i$. Then $H_i$ is not constant, as $p\notin S$. For every edge $\{i,j\}$, $p\nmid H_j$: if $H_j$ is constant, because $p\notin S$, and otherwise by \eqref{eq:sep}, because $p\nmid k_{ij}$. In particular, no edge has both values divisible by $p$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(v), the edges at $i$ contribute $\prod_{j\sim i}(H_i,H_j)_p=(H_i,\Pi_i)_p$, where $\Pi_i=\prod_{j\sim i}H_j$ is not divisible by $p$. By \eqref{eq:star}, $m_i^2\Pi_i\equiv W_i^2\pmod p$, and $p\nmid m_i$, so $\Pi_i$ is a non-zero square modulo $p$, and $(H_i,\Pi_i)_p=(\Pi_i,H_i)_p=1$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:square}. Hence $B_p=1$.

\paragraph{Step 4: cubic reciprocity.} For every place $v$ of $K$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cB}
\mathcal B^{(g)}_v=\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\,\theta_v(J_i,J_j)+\sum_i\theta_v(J_i,c_i)\in\Z/3\Z,
\end{equation}
the first sum over the edges of the $g$-th certificate, and, for a rational prime $p$, put $\mathcal B^{(g)}_p=\sum_{v\mid p}\mathcal B^{(g)}_v$. Applying (H3) to every pair $(J_i,J_j)$ with an edge, multiplied by $w_{ij}$, and to every pair $(J_i,c_i)$, and adding, we find that $\mathcal B^{(g)}_v=0$ for all but finitely many $v$ and $\sum_pB^{(g)}_p=0$ for every $g$, as the complex place contributes $0$. By (H1) and the definition of $\mathrm{cl}_p$, $\mathcal B^{(g)}_p=\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(\mathrm{cl}_p(J_i),\mathrm{cl}_p(J_j))+\sum_i\theta_p(\mathrm{cl}_p(J_i),\mathrm{cl}_p(c_i))$, where $\theta_p$ is the form of Lemma~\ref{lem:cM3} if $p=3$.

\paragraph{Step 5: the cubic certificates at the primes outside $S$.} The set $S$ also contains $3$ and the primes dividing the norms of the constants $c_i$, of the $D_i$, of the $R_{ij}$. Let $p\notin S$, and let $v$ be a place above $p$, with residue field $k_v$; then all these numbers are units at $v$. First, no edge $(i,j)$ has both values divisible by $v$. Indeed, suppose that $v$ divides $J_i$ and $J_j$. If $R_{ij}$ is defined, let $k$ be the linear endpoint used for it and $\{k,l\}=\{i,j\}$, and put $t=\xi$. As $J_k=\varepsilon_k(\lambda_k\eta-s_k(\xi))$ and $\lambda_k$ is a unit at $v$, we have $\eta\equiv s_k(t)/\lambda_k$ modulo $v$, hence $f_k(t)\equiv\lambda_k^dF(x,y)=0$ and $g_{kl}(t)\equiv\lambda_k^{d_l}J_l\equiv0$ modulo $v$. The resultant can be written as $R_{ij}=Af_k+Cg_{kl}$ with polynomials $A$ and $C$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$, so $v$ divides $R_{ij}$, a contradiction. By (H2), a term of \eqref{eq:cB} in which all arguments are units vanishes. Now let $v$ divide $J_i$. Collecting the terms of \eqref{eq:cB} which contain $J_i$, and using (H1) and $\rho_{ji}=-w_{ij}$ for the edges $(j,i)$ with $j<i$, we obtain $\theta_v(J_i,\Pi_i)$ with $\Pi_i=c_i\prod_jJ_j^{\rho_{ij}}$, which is a unit. We claim that $D_i^3\Pi_i\equiv W^3$ modulo $v$ for some $W\in K$ integral at $v$. If $i$ is linear, put $t=\xi$; then $g_{ij}(t)\equiv\lambda_i^{d_j}J_j$ and $f_i(t)\equiv0$ modulo $v$ as above, and \eqref{eq:cstarlin} gives $D_i^3\lambda_i^{E_i}\Pi_i\equiv w_i(t)^3$ modulo $v$, where $E_i=e_i+\sum_j\rho_{ij}d_j$ is divisible by $3$; this gives the claim with $W=w_i(t)/\lambda_i^{E_i/3}$. Otherwise, \eqref{eq:cstar} gives the claim with $W=T_i(x,y)$. Hence the reduction of $\Pi_i$ is a non-zero cube in $k_v$, $\chi(\Pi_i)=0$, and $\theta_v(J_i,\Pi_i)=v(J_i)\chi(\Pi_i)=0$ by (H2). Hence $\mathcal B^{(g)}_v=0$, and $\mathcal B^{(g)}_p=0$.

\paragraph{Step 6: the primes in $S$.} Let $p\in S$, and suppose that $x\equiv a$ and $y\equiv b\pmod{p^k}$. Lemma~\ref{lem:residue} gives, for every vertex $H_i$ of the quadratic graph, a vector $b_i$ and a subspace $D_i$ with $c_p(H_i)\in b_i+D_i$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:cresidue} gives the analogous data for the values $J_i$ of the cubic certificates. If the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope} hold for every quadratic graph and those of Lemma~\ref{lem:cenvelope} hold for every cubic certificate, then $B_p$ and $(\mathcal B^{(g)}_p)_g$ are determined by $a$, $b$ and $k$. Starting from the solutions of \eqref{eq:main} modulo $p$, we refine a residue class $x\equiv a$, $y\equiv b\pmod{p^k}$ to the classes modulo $p^{k+1}$ that contain solutions of \eqref{eq:main} modulo $p^{k+1}$, until all these hypotheses hold, or until no solution remains. Table~\ref{tab:local} lists the resulting terminal classes together with the joint value $((\mathcal B^{(g)}_p)_g;B_p)$; every integer solution lies in one of them. The possible joint values are: for $p=2$, $(1,0;-1)$; for $p=3$, $(0,0;1),\allowbreak (0,1;1),\allowbreak (0,2;1),\allowbreak (1,0;1),\allowbreak (2,2;1)$; for $p=5$, $(0,0;1)$; for $p=7$, $(1,0;-1),\allowbreak (1,0;1),\allowbreak (2,0;1)$; for $p=11$, $(0,0;1)$; for $p=13$, $(0,1;1)$; for $p=19$, $(0,0;1)$; for $p=31$, $(0,0;1)$; for $p=37$, $(0,0;1)$; for $p=43$, $(0,0;1)$.

\paragraph{Step 7: contradiction.} By Steps~4 and~5, $(\sum_{p\in S}\mathcal B^{(g)}_p)_g=(0,0)$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(iv) and Step~3, $\prod_{p\in S}B_p=B_\infty=1$ by Step~2. But by Step~6, the pair $(\textstyle(\sum_{p\in S}\mathcal B^{(g)}_p)_g;\prod_{p\in S}B_p)$ lies in the set consisting of the pairs $(0,0;-1)$, $(0,1;-1)$, $(0,1;1)$, $(0,2;-1)$, $(1,0;-1)$, $(1,0;1)$, $(1,1;-1)$, $(2,0;-1)$, $(2,0;1)$, $(2,1;-1)$, $(2,1;1)$, $(2,2;-1)$, and $(2,2;1)$, which does not contain $(0,0;1)$. This contradiction proves the theorem.
\qed
